People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I just haven’t had much get-up-and-go lately. I used to like choir, and quilting, and going to visit folks, you know, but now I mostly just sit at home with the TV on low. It’s not that I don’t care… I know people are expecting me sometimes. I just can’t seem to make myself go. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it sad, exactly. Maybe kind of… flat some days. Empty, I’m not sure. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to like, such as choir and quilting, when you do them?\n\nPatient: If I actually get myself there… I can enjoy a little bit, I suppose. But that’s the trouble, I just don’t have the push I used to. Choir and quilting and going to see people, they used to come easy, and now most days I just stay home with the TV on low, even when I know folks are expecting me. I don’t mean to be lazy or anything… it’s just hard to get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to enjoy things as much as you used to?\n\nPatient: Not quite as much, I guess. I mean, if I do make it to choir or sit down with my quilting, it’s not awful or anything… but I don’t have that push I used to. Most days it just feels easier to stay home with the TV on low, even when I know folks are expecting me. I don’t like to say that, but… that’s how it’s been.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight lately?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. My clothes seem about the same, I think. I don’t weigh myself much, though… should I be keeping track of that?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything terrible, anyway. I mean, I have some nights where I’m up a bit, but I figured that’s just age or thinking too much. I’m not sure what counts as “trouble sleeping,” though.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've done or not done?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I mean, I feel bad sometimes if I don’t get to things I said I would, or if I let people down a little. But I wouldn’t say it’s all the time or anything serious. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer interested in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I guess a little. I don’t mean I’ve stopped caring completely, but I don’t seem to have the get-up-and-go like I used to. I used to like choir and quilting, and visiting people, and now most days I just stay home with the TV on low. I know folks are expecting me sometimes, and I feel bad about that… but it’s just hard to make myself go. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that normally would bring you joy?\n\nPatient: I can still enjoy things a little once I’m there, I think… but it’s more that I just don’t have the push to get started like I used to. Choir, quilting, visiting folks — those used to feel easy, and now most days I just sit at home with the TV on low. I know people are expecting me sometimes, and I don’t mean to disappoint anybody. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your lack of motivation and interest in activities is interfering with your daily life and relationships?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But I have stepped back some from leading choir and helping with things at the senior center, because I wasn’t keeping up with what I said I’d do. And my church friends check on me more than they used to. I guess they notice when I don’t show up. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been kind of building up since I retired from the school office… a little over two years ago now. It wasn’t all at once, though. Just sort of little by little, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your lack of motivation and interest in activities is mild or more pronounced?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s mild… or maybe mild to moderate, if that’s the right way to put it. It’s not that I can’t enjoy anything at all. I just don’t have the push I used to for choir, quilting, or going to visit folks. Most days I end up sitting at home with the TV on low, even when I know people might be expecting me. I don’t want to make too much of it, but it has been noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings of flatness or lack of motivation are affecting your daily life most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… I guess yes, though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m doing nothing at all. It’s more that I just don’t have the push I used to have for choir or quilting or going to visit folks. A lot of days I end up sitting at home with the TV on low, even when I know somebody’s expecting me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your lack of motivation and interest in activities is impacting your life to a degree that you would consider it a significant problem?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, it has become a problem, though I feel a little embarrassed saying that. It’s mostly that I don’t have the push I used to for choir or quilting, or even just visiting folks. And then most days I’ll just sit at home with the TV on low, even when I know people are expecting me. I don’t know if that counts as significant, but it does get in the way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But that’s the trouble, I just don’t have the push I used to. Choir and quilting and going to see people, they used to come easy, and now most days I just stay home with the TV on low, even when I know folks are expecting me. I mean, if I do make it to choir or sit down with my quilting, it’s not awful or anything… but I don’t have that push I used to. Most days it just feels easier to stay home with the TV on low, even when I know folks are expecting me. I don’t like to say that, but… that’s how it’s been.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight lately?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that normally would bring you joy?\n\nPatient: I can still enjoy things a little once I’m there, I think… but it’s more that I just don’t have the push to get started like I used to. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to like, such as choir and quilting, when you do them?\n\nPatient: If I actually get myself there… I can enjoy a little bit, I suppose. But that’s the trouble, I just don’t have the push I used to. Choir and quilting and going to see people, they used to come easy, and now most days I just stay home with the TV on low, even when I know folks are expecting me. I don’t mean to be lazy or anything… it’s just hard to get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to enjoy things as much as you used to?\n\nPatient: Not quite as much, I guess. I mean, if I do make it to choir or sit down with my quilting, it’s not awful or anything… but I don’t have that push I used to. Most days it just feels easier to stay home with the TV on low, even when I know folks are expecting me. I don’t like to say that, but… that’s how it’s been.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight lately?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. My clothes seem about the same, I think. I don’t weigh myself much, though… should I be keeping track of that?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything terrible, anyway. I mean, I have some nights where I’m up a bit, but I figured that’s just age or thinking too much. I’m not sure what counts as “trouble sleeping,” though.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've done or not done?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I mean, I feel bad sometimes if I don’t get to things I said I would, or if I let people down a little. But I wouldn’t say it’s all the time or anything serious. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer interested in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I guess a little. I don’t mean I’ve stopped caring completely, but I don’t seem to have the get-up-and-go like I used to. I used to like choir and quilting, and visiting people, and now most days I just stay home with the TV on low. I know folks are expecting me sometimes, and I feel bad about that… but it’s just hard to make myself go. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that normally would bring you joy?\n\nPatient: I can still enjoy things a little once I’m there, I think… but it’s more that I just don’t have the push to get started like I used to. Choir, quilting, visiting folks — those used to feel easy, and now most days I just sit at home with the TV on low. I know people are expecting me sometimes, and I don’t mean to disappoint anybody. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your lack of motivation and interest in activities is interfering with your daily life and relationships?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But I have stepped back some from leading choir and helping with things at the senior center, because I wasn’t keeping up with what I said I’d do. And my church friends check on me more than they used to. I guess they notice when I don’t show up. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been kind of building up since I retired from the school office… a little over two years ago now. It wasn’t all at once, though. Just sort of little by little, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your lack of motivation and interest in activities is mild or more pronounced?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s mild… or maybe mild to moderate, if that’s the right way to put it. It’s not that I can’t enjoy anything at all. I just don’t have the push I used to for choir, quilting, or going to visit folks. Most days I end up sitting at home with the TV on low, even when I know people might be expecting me. I don’t want to make too much of it, but it has been noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings of flatness or lack of motivation are affecting your daily life most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… I guess yes, though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m doing nothing at all. It’s more that I just don’t have the push I used to have for choir or quilting or going to visit folks. A lot of days I end up sitting at home with the TV on low, even when I know somebody’s expecting me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your lack of motivation and interest in activities is impacting your life to a degree that you would consider it a significant problem?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, it has become a problem, though I feel a little embarrassed saying that. It’s mostly that I don’t have the push I used to for choir or quilting, or even just visiting folks. And then most days I’ll just sit at home with the TV on low, even when I know people are expecting me. I don’t know if that counts as significant, but it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your lack of motivation and interest in activities is interfering with your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m not working now, so not work exactly… but with responsibilities, yes, a little. I’ve had to step back from leading choir and helping with things at the senior center because I wasn’t keeping up with what I said I’d do. I don’t like admitting that. My church friends check on me more than they used to, which is kind of them, but it makes me feel like I’m not managing as well as I should. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings of flatness or lack of motivation are affecting nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Nearly every day… yes, I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but most days I just don’t have the push I used to have for choir or quilting or going to see folks. I’ll end up sitting at home with the TV on low, even when I know somebody might be expecting me. I’ve had to step back from leading choir and helping with projects at the senior center because I wasn’t keeping up with what I promised people I’d do. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your lack of motivation and interest in activities is interfering with your daily life to the point where you're unable to function most of the time?\n\nPatient: Unable to function most of the time… I don’t know if I’d say it that strong, doctor. I don’t mean to be lazy or anything… it’s just hard to get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to enjoy things as much as you used to?\n\nPatient: Not quite as much, I guess. I mean, if I do make it to choir or sit down with my quilting, it’s not awful or anything… but I don’t have that push I used to. Most days it just feels easier to stay home with the TV on low, even when I know folks are expecting me. I don’t like to say that, but… that’s how it’s been.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight lately?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. My clothes seem about the same, I think. I don’t weigh myself much, though… should I be keeping track of that?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything terrible, anyway. I mean, I have some nights where I’m up a bit, but I figured that’s just age or thinking too much. I’m not sure what counts as “trouble sleeping,” though.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've done or not done?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I mean, I feel bad sometimes if I don’t get to things I said I would, or if I let people down a little. But I wouldn’t say it’s all the time or anything serious. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer interested in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I guess a little. I don’t mean I’ve stopped caring completely, but I don’t seem to have the get-up-and-go like I used to. I used to like choir and quilting, and visiting people, and now most days I just stay home with the TV on low. I know folks are expecting me sometimes, and I feel bad about that… but it’s just hard to make myself go. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that normally would bring you joy?\n\nPatient: I can still enjoy things a little once I’m there, I think… but it’s more that I just don’t have the push to get started like I used to. Choir, quilting, visiting folks — those used to feel easy, and now most days I just sit at home with the TV on low. I know people are expecting me sometimes, and I don’t mean to disappoint anybody. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your lack of motivation and interest in activities is interfering with your daily life and relationships?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But I have stepped back some from leading choir and helping with things at the senior center, because I wasn’t keeping up with what I said I’d do. And my church friends check on me more than they used to. I guess they notice when I don’t show up. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been kind of building up since I retired from the school office… a little over two years ago now. It wasn’t all at once, though. Just sort of little by little, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your lack of motivation and interest in activities is mild or more pronounced?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s mild… or maybe mild to moderate, if that’s the right way to put it. It’s not that I can’t enjoy anything at all. I just don’t have the push I used to for choir, quilting, or going to visit folks. Most days I end up sitting at home with the TV on low, even when I know people might be expecting me. I don’t want to make too much of it, but it has been noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings of flatness or lack of motivation are affecting your daily life most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… I guess yes, though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m doing nothing at all. It’s more that I just don’t have the push I used to have for choir or quilting or going to visit folks. A lot of days I end up sitting at home with the TV on low, even when I know somebody’s expecting me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your lack of motivation and interest in activities is impacting your life to a degree that you would consider it a significant problem?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, it has become a problem, though I feel a little embarrassed saying that. It’s mostly that I don’t have the push I used to for choir or quilting, or even just visiting folks. And then most days I’ll just sit at home with the TV on low, even when I know people are expecting me. I don’t know if that counts as significant, but it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your lack of motivation and interest in activities is interfering with your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m not working now, so not work exactly… but with responsibilities, yes, a little. I’ve had to step back from leading choir and helping with things at the senior center because I wasn’t keeping up with what I said I’d do. I don’t like admitting that. My church friends check on me more than they used to, which is kind of them, but it makes me feel like I’m not managing as well as I should. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings of flatness or lack of motivation are affecting nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Nearly every day… yes, I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but most days I just don’t have the push I used to have for choir or quilting or going to see folks. I’ll end up sitting at home with the TV on low, even when I know somebody might be expecting me. Is that answering what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your lack of motivation and interest in activities is significantly interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, though I wish it didn’t. And my church friends check on me more now than they used to, which is kind of them, but… I guess that means it is getting in the way. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your lack of motivation and interest in activities is interfering with your daily life to the point where you're unable to function most of the time?\n\nPatient: Unable to function most of the time… I don’t know if I’d say it that strong, doctor. I’ve had to step back from leading choir and helping with things at the senior center because I just wasn’t keeping up with what I’d promised people. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I just haven’t had much get-up-and-go lately. I just can’t seem to make myself go. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it sad, exactly. Maybe kind of… flat some days. Empty, I’m not sure. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to like, such as choir and quilting, when you do them?\n\nPatient: If I actually get myself there… I can enjoy a little bit, I suppose. But that’s the trouble, I just don’t have the push I used to. Choir and quilting and going to see people, they used to come easy, and now most days I just stay home with the TV on low, even when I know folks are expecting me. I don’t mean to be lazy or anything… it’s just hard to get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to enjoy things as much as you used to?\n\nPatient: Not quite as much, I guess. I mean, if I do make it to choir or sit down with my quilting, it’s not awful or anything… but I don’t have that push I used to. Most days it just feels easier to stay home with the TV on low, even when I know folks are expecting me. I don’t like to say that, but… that’s how it’s been.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight lately?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. My clothes seem about the same, I think. I don’t weigh myself much, though… should I be keeping track of that?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything terrible, anyway. I mean, I have some nights where I’m up a bit, but I figured that’s just age or thinking too much. I’m not sure what counts as “trouble sleeping,” though.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've done or not done?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I mean, I feel bad sometimes if I don’t get to things I said I would, or if I let people down a little. But I wouldn’t say it’s all the time or anything serious. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer interested in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I guess a little. I don’t mean I’ve stopped caring completely, but I don’t seem to have the get-up-and-go like I used to. I used to like choir and quilting, and visiting people, and now most days I just stay home with the TV on low. I know folks are expecting me sometimes, and I feel bad about that… but it’s just hard to make myself go. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that normally would bring you joy?\n\nPatient: I can still enjoy things a little once I’m there, I think… but it’s more that I just don’t have the push to get started like I used to. Choir, quilting, visiting folks — those used to feel easy, and now most days I just sit at home with the TV on low. I know people are expecting me sometimes, and I don’t mean to disappoint anybody. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your lack of motivation and interest in activities is interfering with your daily life and relationships?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But I have stepped back some from leading choir and helping with things at the senior center, because I wasn’t keeping up with what I said I’d do. And my church friends check on me more than they used to. I guess they notice when I don’t show up. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been kind of building up since I retired from the school office… a little over two years ago now. It wasn’t all at once, though. Just sort of little by little, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your lack of motivation and interest in activities is mild or more pronounced?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s mild… or maybe mild to moderate, if that’s the right way to put it. It’s not that I can’t enjoy anything at all. I just don’t have the push I used to for choir, quilting, or going to visit folks. Most days I end up sitting at home with the TV on low, even when I know people might be expecting me. I don’t want to make too much of it, but it has been noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings of flatness or lack of motivation are affecting your daily life most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… I guess yes, though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m doing nothing at all. It’s more that I just don’t have the push I used to have for choir or quilting or going to visit folks. A lot of days I end up sitting at home with the TV on low, even when I know somebody’s expecting me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your lack of motivation and interest in activities is impacting your life to a degree that you would consider it a significant problem?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, it has become a problem, though I feel a little embarrassed saying that. It’s mostly that I don’t have the push I used to for choir or quilting, or even just visiting folks. And then most days I’ll just sit at home with the TV on low, even when I know people are expecting me. I don’t know if that counts as significant, but it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your lack of motivation and interest in activities is interfering with your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m not working now, so not work exactly… but with responsibilities, yes, a little. I’ve had to step back from leading choir and helping with things at the senior center because I wasn’t keeping up with what I said I’d do. I don’t like admitting that. My church friends check on me more than they used to, which is kind of them, but it makes me feel like I’m not managing as well as I should. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings of flatness or lack of motivation are affecting nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Nearly every day… yes, I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but most days I just don’t have the push I used to have for choir or quilting or going to see folks. I’ll end up sitting at home with the TV on low, even when I know somebody might be expecting me. Is that answering what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your lack of motivation and interest in activities is significantly interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, though I wish it didn’t. I’ve had to step back from leading choir and helping with projects at the senior center because I wasn’t keeping up with what I promised people I’d do. And my church friends check on me more now than they used to, which is kind of them, but… I guess that means it is getting in the way. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your lack of motivation and interest in activities is interfering with your daily life to the point where you're unable to function most of the time?\n\nPatient: Unable to function most of the time… I don’t know if I’d say it that strong, doctor. I still get by some days. But it has gotten in the way. I did have to step back from leading choir and from helping with some senior-center projects because I wasn’t keeping up with what I promised folks. And my church friends check on me more now than they used to. So… yes, it affects my daily life, but maybe not “unable most of the time.” Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your current feelings and lack of motivation as causing significant distress or impairment in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say it does cause some real trouble, even if I try not to make too much of it. I’ve had to step back from leading choir and helping with things at the senior center because I just wasn’t keeping up with what I’d promised people. And my church friends check on me more now than they used to, which is kind of them, but it makes me realize I’m not doing quite as well as I want to be. Is that what you mean by impairment?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your feelings of flatness and lack of motivation are interfering with your daily life to a degree that you would consider it more than mild?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, more than mild, though I don’t like to say it that way. I have had to step back from leading choir and helping with some of the senior-center projects because I wasn’t keeping up with what I’d promised. And my church friends check on me more now than they used to, so I suppose it is affecting things. Is that a fair answer?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
